# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-19

## Recommendation System
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[LSNR: Learned Sparse News Recommendations with Attentive User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23510v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current learned sparse approaches for recommender systems, such as STAR, represent a user by concatenating their interaction history into a single query string. For news, this creates two bottlenecks: concatenation collapses a user's distinct reading interests into one flat query, and, because news articles are long-form text, concatenating several news articles quickly overruns the context window of the language model used as backbone and forces quality-degrading truncation. To address both bottlenecks, we introduce learned sparse news recommendations (LSNR). LSNR encodes each article independently and constructs a sparse user vector by combining learned, attention-based weighting of the per-article sparse representations with a dense-projected expansion at the user level. Experiments on the MIND and EBNeRD datasets show that LSNR significantly outperforms STAR, with these gains holding even in short-history settings where truncation is not a factor. Compared to state-of-the-art dense recommenders, LSNR is competitive in standard settings and it outperforms them when the user history is short. An ablation of the user encoder confirms that learned attention-based aggregation outperforms simpler pooling alternatives. Beyond effectiveness, an additional advantage of LSNR is that it serves from an index an order of magnitude smaller than an uncompressed dense baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables. Accepted at SIGIR-AP 2026</p></details> |
| **[UniRec: Cross-stage Multi-Task Fusion with Preference Optimization for Cascaded Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11052v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial recommender systems use cascaded stages with different candidate spaces, data distributions, and latency constraints. Independently optimizing their multi-task fusion (MTF) modules can cause cross-stage preference inconsistency, while existing cross-stage methods mainly transfer supervision to upstream scoring models and leave fusion modules jointly unoptimized. We propose UniRec, a Unified Cross-stage Recommendation Fusion framework that jointly optimizes pre-ranking and ranking fusion agents. UniRec employs a coupled dual-agent architecture with partially shared embeddings and stage-specific fusion functions. It further introduces Dual-Axis Preference Optimization to combine stage-local preferences with downstream-to-upstream preference transfer. Attribute Group-Relative Regularization (AGRR) mitigates reward-induced distribution drift by normalizing preference advantages within attribute groups. To improve efficiency, Compact Pairwise Preference Aggregation (CPPA) reduces the objective-dependent pairwise complexity, while Confidence-Aware Pair Pruning (CAPP) further reduces the evaluated pair space. Extensive offline and online experiments on a production short-video system demonstrate consistent gains over single-stage fusion and cross-stage coordination baselines. In a nine-week online A/B test, UniRec improves total watch time by $0.675\%$ and app usage time by $0.616\%$, and has been fully deployed in production.</p></details> |  |
| **[Encoded but Exposed: Language Embeddings can Reveal More Than They Should in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21713v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language embeddings are increasingly used in recommenders to represent item content, but their privacy risks remain poorly understood. We study whether item embeddings can reveal item identities when an adversary has access to auxiliary item text from external sources. We show that a simple cosine-similarity attack can recover item identities with high mean reciprocal rank across language models and candidate-set settings on two real-world datasets. Leakage is strongest when the adversary's partial information overlaps with the victim representation, but it also persists through semantic alignment between independently sourced descriptions. We further show that item leakage can propagate to user re-identification from sparse ratings. Finally, we evaluate obfuscated descriptions as a mitigation and find a clear leakage-utility tradeoff.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Quantum Machine Learning Approach to Content-Based Media Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21562v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We use quantum machine learning techniques to develop a content-based media recommender system and evaluate its performance on TV programmes using textual descriptions from one week of BBC iPlayer data. Grounded in the Quantum Natural Language Processing framework (QNLP), programme synopses are mapped to variational quantum circuits using their semantic structure. The parameters of these circuits are trained via a supervised pairwise similarity learning task. We benchmark the quantum recommender against a classical BiLSTM baseline trained on the same dataset and objective. While both models achieve comparable performance on the similarity learning task, the quantum model requires significantly fewer parameters (47k vs. 17.8M). When the BiLSTM is constrained to a matching parameter budget, its performance degrades substantially (by 3.63 percentage points in Mean Average Precision (MAP)), indicating that the structured quantum representation provides a highly parameter-efficient alternative in this setting. We demonstrate the feasibility of executing the trained models on quantum hardware by deploying the circuits on Quantinuum's System Model H2 (and accompanying emulator) using a SWAP-test-based protocol. The results confirm that the similarity signal is preserved under realistic, noisy conditions. Taken together, these findings highlight the potential of variational quantum circuits for recommendation tasks and represent a practical step toward their real-world deployment.</p></details> |  |
| **[RecSoup: Efficient Model Merging Approach for Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21220v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems often need to balance multiple conflicting objectives. Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation (CMOR) enables dynamic adjustment of objective trade-offs at inference time without retraining, adapting to shifting user or system requirements. Existing CMOR approaches typically rely on output-level reranking or parameter-generating modules, which either limit the scope of feasible trade-offs or incur substantial training and inference cost. Model merging provides a promising alternative by reusing multiple objective-specific models via parameter combination. However, its direct application to recommender systems is challenging due to the lack of a shared linear regime and cross-objective interference. To address these issues, we propose RecSoup, a lightweight two-stage approach for effective CMOR via specialized model merging. RecSoup first mitigates parameter interference across objectives through a pruning strategy that jointly considers objective-specific importance and cross-objective relevance. It then performs adaptive parameter merging via a learnable preference-to-coefficient mapping, allowing dynamic trade-off control at inference time without joint retraining or additional generative modules. Theoretically, we prove that RecSoup outperforms the parameter generation paradigm in effectiveness and efficiency, as it can fully navigate the Pareto front without smoothness constraints. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that RecSoup achieves improved Pareto front quality and effective controllability while delivers 165--228x faster inference-time latency than parameter generation approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/35798642/RECSOUP.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CIKM 2026</p></details> |
| **[From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26277v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.</p></details> | <details><summary>Dianj...</summary><p>Dianjing Fan and Yao Li equally contributed to this work. 7 pages, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[On the Impact of Graph Neural Networks in Recommender Systems: A Topological Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07384v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recommender systems, user-item interactions can be modeled as a bipartite graph, where user and item nodes are connected by undirected edges. This graph-based view has motivated the rapid adoption of graph neural networks (GNNs), which often outperform collaborative filtering (CF) methods such as latent factor models, deep neural networks, and generative strategies. Yet, despite their empirical success, the reasons why GNNs offer systematic advantages over other CF approaches remain only partially understood. This monograph advances a topology-centered perspective on GNN-based recommendation. We argue that a comprehensive understanding of these models' performance should consider the structural properties of user-item graphs and their interaction with GNN architectural design. To support this view, we introduce a formal taxonomy that distills common modeling patterns across eleven representative GNN-based recommendation approaches and consolidates them into a unified conceptual pipeline. We further formalize thirteen classical and topological characteristics of recommendation datasets and reinterpret them through the lens of graph machine learning. Using these definitions, we analyze the considered GNN-based recommender architectures to assess how and to what extent they encode such properties. Building on this analysis, we derive an explanatory framework that links measurable dataset characteristics to model behavior and performance. Taken together, this monograph re-frames GNN-based recommendation through its topological underpinnings and outlines open theoretical, data-centric, and evaluation challenges for the next generation of topology-aware recommender systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Foundations and Trends in Information Retrieval</p></details> |
| **[Bridging the Topological Divide: Dual-Branch Graph Distillation with Semantic Priors for Robust Cold-Start Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19578v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Collaborative Filtering models have achieved remarkable success in recommendation systems. However, they inherently suffer from a catastrophic representation collapse in strict cold-start scenarios due to the topological isolation of unobserved items. While integrating multi-modal semantic features offers a potential remedy, directly mapping isolated semantic attributes into a globally topology-driven collaborative space frequently precipitates representation degradation and optimization conflicts. To bridge this fundamental structural divide, we propose DualGCN, an innovative Dual-Branch Graph Knowledge Distillation Framework. Operating under a decoupled two-stage training paradigm, DualGCN first constructs a robust collaborative teacher network where item representations are anchored by a topology-smoothed semantic prior, effectively reshaping the latent manifold. Subsequently, a semantic student branch employs a residual mapping mechanism and a magnitude-agnostic cosine distillation objective to achieve high-fidelity cross-modal alignment without disrupting established collaborative signals. Furthermore, we introduce a dynamic norm reconstruction mechanism to rigorously calibrate the physical scales of novel and warm items during global retrieval. Extensive experiments across three real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that DualGCN significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in strict cold-start scenarios while comprehensively preserving recommendation accuracy for highly active items.</p></details> |  |
| **[xLightGCN: Explainable Graph Neural Network Based Recommendation Systems with Most Influential Peers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19446v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommendation systems, systems that predict a user's preference for items in order to curate that user's feed, are a ubiquitous and central feature in online social networks. While they are a central piece of our modern digital infrastructure, many modern approaches are opaque black boxes, making them difficult to audit, understand, and assess the trustworthiness of. In this paper we present an exact, graph-based approach for generating explanations for decisions made by recommendation systems. We argue that in the risk-sensitive sociotechnical context of user-facing AI such as recommendation systems, explainability is a key pillar for establishing the trustworthiness of models and validating their effectiveness. We propose a technique built on LightGCN like architectures, an influential and popular class of recommendation models. We describe our approach and argue that it provides an appropriate assurance mechanism for these AI systems leading to specific advantages, including interpretable explanations and a theoretically guaranteed measure of explanation fidelity. We conclude by discussing limitations and directions for future research.</p></details> |  |
| **[Prompt or Policy? Optimizing Physician Slate Presentations with LLM User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18309v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Choosing the right physician is a key challenge for patients seeking medical care. Physician recommendation systems identify relevant doctors, but patients still find it difficult to compare their profiles to make a decision. We formulate slate presentation optimization: given a patient's medical case and a fixed, ranked list of physicians, we generate short, case-specific summaries that make doctors easier to compare, while remaining grounded in the information in their profiles. To optimize physician presentations without relying on real patient interactions, we develop an offline framework that uses persona-conditioned LLMs to simulate patient choices. We generate patient personas from attributes extracted from each medical case and evaluate presentations for factual accuracy, relevance, naturalness and simulated patient choice. We also use a second LLM judge, unseen during optimization, to assess simulator-specific overfitting. We compare automatic prompt-level optimization with GEPA, parameter-level optimization with GRPO, and their combination across two model sizes. GRPO substantially outperforms GEPA in satisfying the full set of constraints, and its gains in simulated conversion persist under the unseen judge. GEPA alone brings smaller improvements, but using its optimized prompt to initialize GRPO speeds up training and achieves the highest simulated conversion. These results show that prompt-level and parameter-level optimization play complementary roles in improving how physician recommendations are presented.</p></details> |  |
| **[SimInter: Evidence-Grounded User Simulation with Interview-Based Profile Enrichment for Conversational Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18308v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational Recommender Systems (CRSs) require reliable user simulators for cost-effective evaluation. LLM-based simulators have made realistic simulation possible but typically condition on profiles derived from explicit ratings, demographics, and static sources capturing surface preferences while omitting implicit behavioral characteristics such as decision-making style, aspect-level preferences, and domain expertise. We propose SimInter, a pipeline that elicits these attributes via synthetic interviews between two LLM agents, grounded in the user's statistical profile and historical conversational evidence. Before profile enrichment, an audit agent explicitly flags potential fabrication and stereotyping at the turn level. We instantiate and evaluate SimInter on movie recommendation. We simulate 300 ReDial users and, on a matched subset of 100 users, compare SimInter with a statistics-only baseline: SimInter significantly increases the diversity of expressed preference aspects (filtered aspect entropy: 1.619 vs. 0.859; paired d = 0.96, Holm-adjusted p < 0.001). This gain persists when the simulator converses with two trained recommenders, KBRD and UniCRS, under two independent evaluation judges (+0.28 to +0.56 nats; all p < 0.001). Human evaluation finds 94.3% of enriched-attribute claims supported or partially supported, while human judgments agree with 95.2% of the auditor's binary filtering decisions on a stratified sample.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 11 figures, 12 tables. Erfan Shahabi and Amirhossein Roshandel contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[LLMRec: Benchmarking Large Language Models on Recommendation Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.12241v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recently, the fast development of Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT has significantly advanced NLP tasks by enhancing the capabilities of conversational models. However, the application of LLMs in the recommendation domain has not been thoroughly investigated. To bridge this gap, we propose LLMRec, a LLM-based recommender system designed for benchmarking LLMs on various recommendation tasks. Specifically, we benchmark several popular off-the-shelf LLMs, such as ChatGPT, LLaMA, ChatGLM, on five recommendation tasks, including rating prediction, sequential recommendation, direct recommendation, explanation generation, and review summarization. Furthermore, we investigate the effectiveness of supervised finetuning to improve LLMs' instruction compliance ability. The benchmark results indicate that LLMs displayed only moderate proficiency in accuracy-based tasks such as sequential and direct recommendation. However, they demonstrated comparable performance to state-of-the-art methods in explainability-based tasks. We also conduct qualitative evaluations to further evaluate the quality of contents generated by different models, and the results show that LLMs can truly understand the provided information and generate clearer and more reasonable results. We aspire that this benchmark will serve as an inspiration for researchers to delve deeper into the potential of LLMs in enhancing recommendation performance. Our codes, processed data and benchmark results are available at https://github.com/williamliujl/LLMRec.</p></details> |  |
| **[Web Scale on a Budget: Tail-Aware Retrieval Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17986v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A common practice in instance retrieval is to grow the gallery size to improve a benchmark's discrimination capability. One prominent example is ILIAS, which adds up to 100 million distractor images. The prevailing assumption is that faithful evaluation demands web scale, and that indexing all 100M images through the backbone is the unavoidable price of a comparable number. That price is steep: hundreds of GPU-hours and ~200 GB of descriptors per model, paid again for every method or ablation. We show the web-scale indexing price is largely avoidable: evaluation itself obeys a scaling law. The number of distractors that outrank a positive is Binomial in the gallery size. We define the per-query retrieval hardness, a gallery-scale agnostic quantity that yields a retrieval metric at any scale, and demonstrate it can be derived from a small random sample. The underlying sampled-rank machinery is long established in recommender-system and biometric evaluation, but to our knowledge has never been adapted to visual retrieval. We make the law per-query and estimable, and validate it at true 100M scale on ILIAS and INSTRE, and on the full 759k-distractor Google Landmarks v2 pool. Concretely, within a 5% sample (a flat 20x less indexing), every backbone's tail-aware (EVT) estimate has converged onto the large-scale mAP, to within ~0.01 (mean absolute error 0.005), across five backbones, three benchmarks, and two distractor pools (mAP@1000 at 100M on ILIAS/INSTRE; the official mAP@100 at the full 759k pool on GLDv2); the recommender-systems count baseline over-predicts everywhere (+0.037 to +0.068). This is an order of magnitude better than naively reporting the metric on the same subset (+0.08 to +0.13). We release code and the per-query hardness to facilitate faster, cheaper, and greener retrieval evaluation.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 8 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[Mitigating Popularity Bias in Sequential Recommendations with Sparse Prior-Regularized Objective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17823v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Popularity bias is a well-known challenge in recommender systems, often leading to the over-exposure of popular items and reduced catalog coverage. In this work, we propose PR-entmax, a sparse prior-regularized objective that makes popularity correction context-dependent. We derive our method from a generalized variational principle in which an item popularity prior is incorporated directly into a sparse probability mapping, yielding a popularity-aware active support: the set of items receiving non-zero probabilities and hence non-zero gradients is determined jointly by contextual relevance and item popularity. As a result, the prior affects not only item probabilities, but also which items participate in gradient updates during training. The method requires no architectural changes and can be integrated into existing training pipelines by modifying only the loss function. At inference time, the prior term is removed, and items are ranked by the learned contextual relevance alone. Experiments across multiple benchmark datasets and sequential recommendation architectures show that PR-entmax consistently reduces popularity bias while preserving or improving recommendation relevance, advancing the relevance-debiasing trade-off.</p></details> |  |
| **[Nonparametric Kernel Clustering with Bandit Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07535v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clustering with bandit feedback refers to the problem of partitioning the arms of an $N$-armed stochastic bandit according to their underlying distributions, based on noisy observations collected sequentially and adaptively. This setting has attracted growing interest for its applicability in recommendation systems, crowdsourcing, and adaptive trials. Existing works rely on the strong assumption that arm distributions are sub-Gaussian, which can only distinguish arms whose distributions differ in mean - missing realistic settings where distributional differences lie in shape or higher moments. % We introduce a framework of \emph{nonparametric clustering with bandit feedback}, free of any parametric assumptions regarding the arm distributions. We adopt a kernel-based approach and reformulate the problem as clustering arms according to their kernel mean embeddings in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). Building on this formulation, we introduce the \kabc\ algorithm, prove that it is $δ$-PAC, and derive a non-asymptotic high-probability upper bound on its sampling complexity. The bound is governed by a variance-aware signal-to-noise ratio defined as the minimum over pairs of arms in distinct clusters of the ratio between their squared MMD and their RKHS variance. \kabc\ achieves instance-dependent guarantees while being adaptive to this unknown ratio. We validate our approach empirically on synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating that \kabc\ succeeds in distributional clustering settings where mean-based methods fail.</p></details> |  |
| **[FindRec: Stein-Guided Entropic Flow for Multi-Modal Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.04651v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern recommendation systems face significant challenges in processing multimodal sequential data, particularly in temporal dynamics modeling and information flow coordination. Traditional approaches struggle with distribution discrepancies between heterogeneous features and noise interference in multimodal signals. We propose \textbf{FindRec}~ (\textbf{F}lexible unified \textbf{in}formation \textbf{d}isentanglement for multi-modal sequential \textbf{Rec}ommendation), introducing a novel "information flow-control-output" paradigm. The framework features two key innovations: (1) A Stein kernel-based Integrated Information Coordination Module (IICM) that theoretically guarantees distribution consistency between multimodal features and ID streams, and (2) A cross-modal expert routing mechanism that adaptively filters and combines multimodal features based on their contextual relevance. Our approach leverages multi-head subspace decomposition for routing stability and RBF-Stein gradient for unbiased distribution alignment, enhanced by linear-complexity Mamba layers for efficient temporal modeling. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate FindRec's superior performance over state-of-the-art baselines, particularly in handling long sequences and noisy multimodal inputs. Our framework achieves both improved recommendation accuracy and enhanced model interpretability through its modular design. The implementation code is available anonymously online for easy reproducibility~\footnote{https://github.com/Applied-Machine-Learning-Lab/FindRec}.</p></details> | Accepted by KDD 2025 |
| **[An Analysis of Role Semantics in Directed Dynamic Graph Modeling for Edge Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23135v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge classification on directed dynamic graphs is critical for understanding evolving user-item interactions and behavioral patterns in recommender systems and social platforms. Existing dynamic graph architectures typically process these interactions using shared parameters for nodes with semantically distinct roles, including source, destination, query, and neighbor. While abundant supervision in tasks such as link prediction provides sufficient gradient signal for models to implicitly deduce role semantics and overcome this conflation, there are often far fewer labels available for edge classification. We hypothesize that in such regimes, the ability of models to implicitly separate roles is restricted, degrading downstream performance. To empirically investigate this relationship, we introduce an experimental framework for sequence-based models that explicitly disentangles functional roles using targeted embeddings and an asymmetric pretraining objective. By providing architectural priors for these roles, the framework serves as an evaluative vehicle to isolate and measure the resulting performance delta across a range of label budgets. Our empirical evaluation demonstrates that this explicit role disentanglement yields substantial and consistent relative performance gains over baselines. Furthermore, representational analyses reveal that the mechanism by which the framework provides these benefits is a bifurcation of source and destination latent spaces that improves linear separability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tackling the Modality Inhibitance Effect with Wavelet-based Modality Integration in Multi-modal Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16229v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Harnessing rich modality features has recently gained prominence in multi-modal recommendation systems (MRSs). To delineate user modality preferences, existing works focus on modality fusion, which can be achieved via attentive summation or concatenation. In this work, we introduce the modality inhibitance effect - a phenomenon which limits existing MRSs when these models transit from dual-modal to tri-modal configurations resulting in significant performance degradation. In particular, apart from the modal-specific contamination encapsulated in the newly introduced modality, the inclusion of new features results in excessive informational redundancy, thereby severely impeding effective integration within current approaches. To address the modality inhibitance effect, we propose a new multi-scale modality wavelet (MMWave) multi-modal recommender, which aims to fully capitalize on essential modality features and suppress modal-specific noise. This is acheived by employing wavelet decomposition from a multi-scale (time \& frequency) perspective, ensuring that only essential (non-redundant) features of each modality are extracted. We then account for the latent associations between item modality features by designing a new structure-aware multi-modal graph learning module that captures high-order latent structural information pertaining to items. To modulate the relative contributions of modality features and achieve effective integration, we then propose a new adaptive modality regulator component that employs a new cross-weight integration strategy. Experiments conducted on four real-world datasets validate the efficacy of our proposed model. The source code for this work has been made publicly available at https://github.com/kennethorq/MMWave.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in IEEE Transactions on Knowledge and Data Engineering (TKDE) 2026</p></details> |
| **[Heterogeneous Agentic Coevolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16078v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in self-improving LLM agents have demonstrated the potential of AI systems that drive their own intelligence. However, many domain-specific applications rely on specialized foundation models that possess strong domain expertise but remain dependent on human-designed improvements. Extending recursive self-improvement to these models presents a key challenge: as a specialist improves, the strategies used to advance it must also adapt. To address this challenge, we propose Heterogeneous Agentic CoEvolution (HACE), a framework that jointly evolves specialized foundation models and LLM agents through reciprocal learning. HACE improves the specialist through supervised learning while training the agent with on-policy reinforced self-training, guided by verified performance gains on the current specialist. The agent's exploration, in turn, informs subsequent specialist training, enabling both models to adapt to each other's progress. To sustain this reciprocal learning, HACE further employs evolutionary search to optimize their interaction harness, adapting how the models exchange information and select training experiences as their capabilities evolve. We evaluate HACE across three challenging domains: recommendation systems, protein function prediction in biology, and crystal stability prediction in material discovery. We show that HACE outperforms the evaluated self-improving methods on the primary task metrics across all three domains. These results shed light on heterogeneous coevolution as a universal approach to improving specialized foundation models through reciprocal interaction with a learning agent.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Citation Recommendation Without Graph Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15832v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Resolving correct citations can be a time-consuming task for research writers. We address local citation recommendation (LCR), where the goal is to retrieve appropriate references for a given sentence. Web tools generally depend on the existing citation graph, which may bias results toward citation count or more recent publications. LCR research using full paper texts has been limited by the lack of publicly available data. We present a new dataset, the Local Citation Recommendation Corpus (LCRC), containing 14,735 scientific statements derived from astrophysics review papers, each with one or more citation targets whose full text is accessible via API. Using this dataset we systematically compare retrieval methods and develop a citation recommendation system that avoids citation graph bias while achieving 8.0% improvement in Recall@25 and 6.9% improvement in NDCG@25 over dense retrieval baselines without model fine-tuning. To support reproducibility, we make our code and dataset publicly available.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at WASP 2026, co-located with AACL-IJCNLP 2026. 16 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/adsabs/LCRC Code: https://github.com/benjaminb/citeline</p></details> |
| **[UNISON: A Unified Framework for Learning from Scored Bags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15564v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce UNISON, a unified framework that bridges multiple instance learning and recommendation systems by treating both as instances of learning from scored bag collections of (item, score) pairs. Unlike traditional approaches that address bag-level classification and personalized recommendation as separate problems, UNISON decomposes learning into three interconnected stages: (1) learning universal item representations, (2) modeling bag-specific preference patterns through lightweight personalized parameters, and (3) inferring bag-level characteristics from learned preferences. We justify the architectural choice of linear bag-specific scoring through a first-order analysis: when item embeddings are sufficiently smooth in the score, a linear function approximates the score up to a Taylor remainder controlled by the curvature of the underlying preference function. For cold-start scenarios, we show that the cold-bag fitting problem reduces to ridge-less linear regression with a closed-form solution, and provide convergence guarantees for the iterative variant. Empirically, UNISON matches or outperforms state-of-the-art approaches across diverse domains: entertainment (books, movies), immunology (T cell receptor (TCR) based classification), and microbiome, offering superior computational efficiency and interpretability. A controlled synthetic experiment further confirms that the bag-specific parameters ($w_j, b_j$) recover the latent factors that drive item scores even when the projection $φ$ has no access to the original scoring mechanism (AUC > 0.998). UNISON is available at https://github.com/louzounlab/UNISON.</p></details> |  |
| **[Federated Semantic Residuals for Multimodal Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15505v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated Recommendation Systems (FedRSs) learn collaborative scores from interaction histories on device and rank items by them, so a higher score means a higher rank. Item text and images add semantic information that can reorder the list. Existing Federated Multimodal Recommenders (FedMRs) fuse this content into their models, which can obscure how content reorders the list. We therefore propose the SEmantic Residual Adapter (SERA), which keeps content out of an existing FedRS and adds semantic residual scores to its collaborative scores. Content can then lift an item above another only when its semantic residual score exceeds the other's by more than their collaborative score gap. This score grows with the item's content similarity to the user's interaction history and is capped at a residual bound. We prove that for any content, an item can enter the list only if its collaborative score is within twice this bound of the lowest one in the list. A larger bound lets content lift more items, including irrelevant ones, so the bound trades off how far content reaches against accuracy. More generally, there exist instances where any committee of size $k$ has at least a $\frac{2}{(k+1)}-ε$ fraction of the electorate preferring another alternative, for arbitrarily small $ε$. Our code is available at https://github.com/youtube/static-constraint-decoding.</p></details> | <details><summary>KDD 2...</summary><p>KDD 2026 camera-ready</p></details> |

| **[GISTBench: Evaluating LLM User Understanding via Evidence-Based Interest Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29112v2)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce GISTBench, a benchmark for evaluating Large Language Models' (LLMs) ability to understand users from their interaction histories in recommendation systems. We release a synthetic dataset constructed on real user interactions on a global short-form video platform. Our dataset contains both implicit and explicit engagement signals and rich textual descriptions. We validate our dataset fidelity against user surveys, and evaluate eight open-weight LLMs spanning 7B to 235B parameters, together with three proprietary frontier models (GPT-5, Claude 4.6, and Gemini 3.5 Flash). | **[Optimizing Effective Training Time for Large-Scale Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02057v1)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lifecycle overhead silently consumes accelerator capacity across large-scale recommendation training fleets. | **[Recommendation Systems for Exploratory Data Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39412v2)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A large class of data-centric tasks is exploratory, where users iteratively steer workflows, refining subjective goals as new insights emerge. These Exploratory Data Tasks (EDTs) are performed by millions of users with varying levels of expertise to understand unfamiliar data, discover trends, and identify evidence that informs critical decision-making. However, a key challenge in EDTs is the enormous space of possible actions that one can take at each step: users struggle to choose among thousands of joins, transformations, and aggregations, causing "exploration paralysis". Because EDT workflows are interconnected, each choice impacts subsequent exploration, and suboptimal choices can lead to inefficiency, missed insights, confirmation bias, and incomplete coverage. This calls for intelligent recommendations that efficiently guide users toward optimal EDT actions. We envision recommendation as a core capability of data systems, proactively guiding users toward promising actions and thereby lowering the barrier to exploratory data tasks. EDT recommendation is challenging because the action space is combinatorial and actions are data-dependent, which require costly materialization. Moreover, recommendation often involves bundles or sequences of actions across interdependent tasks, requiring coordination across tasks. In this paper, we present our vision of EDT recommendation systems along two axes: single-task vs. multi-task settings and single-action vs. multi-action recommendations. We outline a research agenda that progresses from recommending individual EDT actions to constrained bundles and sequences of actions, and ultimately to coordinated recommendations across interconnected EDTs. We identify research directions for incorporating various contexts (user, data, task, and ecosystem), addressing efficiency challenges, and coordinating across tasks.</p></details> |  |

| **[Two-Sided State-Space Models for Sequential Recommendation with Non-Random Multimodal Review Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00165v2)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Two-sided digital platforms are inherently dynamic: user preferences shift, item popularity evolves, and reviews both reflect and drive these changes. | **[Towards More Efficient, Robust, Instance-adaptive, and Generalizable Sequential Decision making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09192v5)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The primary goal of my Ph.D. study is to develop provably efficient and practical algorithms for data-driven sequential decision-making under uncertainty. My work focuses on reinforcement learning (RL), multi-armed bandits, and their applications, including recommendation systems, computer networks, video analytics, and large language models (LLMs). Sequential decision-making methods, such as bandits and RL, have demonstrated remarkable success - ranging from outperforming human players in complex games like Atari and Go to advancing robotics, recommendation systems, and fine-tuning LLMs. Despite these successes, many established algorithms rely on idealized models that can fail under model misspecifications or adversarial perturbations, particularly in settings where accurate prior knowledge of the underlying model class is unavailable or where malicious users operate within dynamic systems. These challenges are pervasive in real-world applications, where robust and adaptive solutions are critical. Furthermore, while worst-case guarantees provide theoretical reliability, they often fail to capture instance-dependent performance, which can lead to more efficient and practical solutions. Another key challenge lies in generalizing to new, unseen environments, a crucial requirement for deploying these methods in dynamic and unpredictable settings. To address these limitations, my research aims to develop more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable sequential decision-making algorithms for both reinforcement learning and bandits. Towards this end, I focus on developing more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable for both general reinforcement learning (RL) and bandits.</p></details> | Ph.D. Thesis |

The observation model inherits the logistic bandit challenge of handling the problem-dependent constant $κ$, which accounts for the non-linearity of the link function and can grow arbitrarily large. TIDE treats memory as a capacity-constrained population of experiences: temporal and semantic credit assignment estimates contextual fitness, while responsibility credit distributes outcome signals according to the memories referenced during generation. These signals are then used to reinforce, crossover, mutate, or evict memories. No comparable metric-sensitive guarantee is known for the transportation algorithms of Arndt et al.\ or Harada and Itoh. Beyond these competitive guarantees, RG provides a simple explanation for its decisions. It favors the natural nearest-neighbor assignment and, for suitable parameters, departs from this choice only when it identifies a reassignment that reduces the cost of its maintained auxiliary matching, thereby correcting accumulated assignment costs. | **[Efficient Offline Learning of Ranking Policies via Top-$k$ Policy Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36740v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many recommender systems such as for e-commerce and news platforms aim to provide users with rankings they are likely to interact with. The regression-based approach, on the other hand, estimates the expected reward using conventional machine learning methods, avoiding variance issues but potentially suffering from severe bias. Specifically, R-POD decomposes a ranking policy into a first-stage policy for selecting top-$k$ actions and a second-stage policy for choosing the bottom actions given the top-$k$ actions. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Structural Causal Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08682v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce structural causal bottleneck models (SCBMs), a novel class of structural causal models in which causal effects between high-dimensional variables are mediated by low-dimensional summary statistics, or *bottlenecks*. SCBMs provide a flexible framework for mechanism-specific, target-dependent dimension reduction while remaining estimable via standard learning algorithms. We prove that bottleneck variables are identifiable up to bijection from observational data, and validate this experimentally. We then study causal effect estimation in linear SCBMs under three standard adjustment strategies. For instrumental variables, we identify a population-level non-identifiability of naïve two-stage least squares when all variables are high-dimensional, and show that bottleneck representations restore identification. For mediation and backdoor adjustment, we prove that conditioning on bottleneck variables yields valid effect estimates with explicit finite-sample efficiency gains over conditioning on full high-dimensional variables. We argue that SCBMs provide a principled alternative to existing causal dimension reduction frameworks such as causal representation learning and causal abstractions.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Synthetic Image Oversampling for Rare-Class Representation Learning in Deep Facial Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23730v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent deep facial phenotyping methods can support the recognition of genetic syndromes, but patient images are often scarce and unevenly distributed across disorders. Synthetic facial images offer a way to supplement the training data without collecting additional patient photographs. It is therefore necessary to examine both how much synthetic augmentation to use and which disorders benefit. We investigate disease-specific synthetic oversampling using images from the Phenotypic Disease Image Database to augment a subset of the GestaltMatcher Database. We compare increasing augmentation levels in an image-based model, GestaltMatcher-Arc, and a multimodal model, GestaltMML, while keeping the real training and patient-disjoint evaluation partitions fixed. Repeated training runs assess overall and disease-specific accuracy, and an exploratory embedding analysis examines changes in class compactness and separation. Augmentation improves mean recognition accuracy in both training setups, although gains remain modest in GestaltMatcher-Arc and continue across the tested levels in GestaltMML. These aggregate improvements are accompanied by uneven disease-level responses, including reduced accuracy for some disorders. In the embedding analysis, disorders with fewer real training images tend to gain more class separation, but geometric changes do not consistently track classification performance. Together, the findings support synthetic oversampling for limited-data facial phenotyping and emphasize the need to assess its amount and benefit for individual disorders within each training setup.</p></details> | <details><summary>BIBM ...</summary><p>BIBM 2026 ML4BMI Workshop</p></details> |

This makes saliency analysis particularly informative: the resulting attribution maps highlight visual cues and structural irregularities characteristic of each model's outputs. | **[Who Can Change Course? Inputs include compute, data, talent, research, capital, deployment and institutional experimentation. Potential losses include coordination failures, administrative duplication, energy constraints, regulatory uncertainty and limited organizational uptake. Regulation also supports learning through evaluation, trusted data access, interoperability and safety standards. The argument is that sovereignty depends on converting distributed resources and knowledge into usable, coordinated and responsibly evaluated capability. RGB-specific, infrared-specific, and fused identity representations are maintained as jointly accessible appearance states. The individual components are intentionally simple, focusing evaluation on reliability-controllable appearance organization rather than elaborate fusion or learned reliability-estimation modules. RCA-MOT sets new SOTA results on VT-MOT and UniRTL, improving the previous best HOTA by 5.402/6.075 points and IDF1 by 11.549/12.789 points, respectively, while post-hoc analyses further validate reliability as an informative control signal. | **[TriFold: Folding irregular 3D Gaussians into structured latents for 3D Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23050v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent progress in 3D generation has motivated growing interest in compact generative representations of complex 3D scenes. Within this framework, we prove an impossibility result: no interpretation can simultaneously satisfy a collection of desirable properties while remaining non-circular. Under a circular interpretation, every model that fits the observational distribution is interventionally valid, so no experiment can falsify it. We analyze several non-circular alternatives and discuss implications for falsification, causal discovery, causal representation learning, and causal abstraction. More broadly, our framework provides a way to make assumptions about the connections between actions and interventions explicit and to reason formally about the empirical content of causal models.</p></details> |  |

The completed design contains 384 runs: six conditions, four held-out targets, and four run seeds per dataset. Checkpoints are selected using only the three source-validation environments. Inference first averages paired seed differences within each held-out target; targets, rather than 16 seed-by-target observations, are the per-dataset clusters. The full implementation's equal-dataset macro point estimate is 1.61 percentage points below native DomainBed ERM (95% clustered CI [-3.17, -0.04]). All four dataset point estimates favor ERM, but an exact four-dataset sign-flip test gives p = .125 and Holm-adjusted p = .250; we therefore claim no adjusted evidence of a difference. The cleaner domain-specificity contrast, separate learned source scales without their variance penalty versus one shared learned source scale, is -0.13 pp (95% CI [-0.53, 0.27]). Its 90% CI lies within a revision-stage $\pm 1$ pp smallest effect of practical interest (TOST p = .003), supporting equivalence only for the source-selected suite macro, not every dataset, target, or final-step endpoint. These findings separate three testable aspects of predictive physiological representation learning: information access, supervised future structure, and transfer.</p></details> | under review |
| **[From Simulated Fluids to Living Tissue: Zero-Shot Foundation Model Transfer to Perturbed Embryos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16236v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Morphogenesis is driven by the interplay of genetic regulation and tissue mechanics. In vivo video data is limited, particularly under genetic mutations and environmental perturbations, making generalization beyond the most representative conditions in the dataset challenging. However, at tissue scales, the dynamics of Drosophila can be described as a continuum flow, motivating the usage of representations learned across other physical systems as priors for developmental dynamics. We investigate finetuning Walrus, a Continuum Dynamics Foundation Model pretrained across a broad set of simulated continuous systems, on tissue velocity fields from only 12 wild-type Drosophila blastoderm videos. We evaluate autoregressive prediction both in-distribution and zero-shot under unseen temperature and genetic perturbations. Zero-shot refers to evaluation on perturbation conditions never seen during wild-type finetuning, without any additional training. In-distribution, a Fourier Neural Operator model trained from scratch achieves best performance on VRMSE. However, Walrus (CRPS) is the strongest model under the most severe distribution shift of unseen samples of mutated embryos and temperature conditions. Interestingly, predictions capture the shape of the mean surface tissue velocity trajectory and its separation between 17°C and 27°C, including the timing of the peak at Germ Band Extension onset, a biologically meaningful milestone of embryo development. Our results provide preliminary support that representations learned from different dynamical systems can transfer to experimental morphogenesis in developmental regimes, provided they admit a continuum description. | **[VGGA: Geometry-Grounded Autoencoder for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16204v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoencoders are the core component of latent video generative models, compressing high-dimensional videos into compact latent spaces for downstream generation. Building on this insight, we propose \textbf{V}ideo \textbf{G}eometry \textbf{G}rounded \textbf{A}utoencoder (\textbf{VGGA}), a geometry-aware video autoencoder that explicitly incorporates geometric constraints into the reconstruction process. Specifically, VGGA grounds the learned representations along three complementary geometric dimensions: \emph{Structural Depth}, \emph{Motion Correspondence}, and \emph{Projective Warping}. These objectives are unified within a Video Geometry Decoder, which employs a shared spatio-temporal upsampling trunk to jointly optimize geometric learning and RGB reconstruction. Through these geometry-grounded objectives, VGGA encourages the latent representations to capture the underlying 3D structure and motion of dynamic scenes. | **[Sera: Semantic Representation Aggregation with Knowledge and LLM for Reliable and Interpretable Battery Health Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11567v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Battery state of health (SoH) forecasting is important for battery management, but remains challenging due to nonlinear degradation and heterogeneity across batteries. In this paper, we propose Sera, a semantic representation aggregation framework that complements temporal modelling with degradation semantics. Guided by battery domain expertise, Sera extracts degradation semantics from time series and constructs two complementary representations using rule-based knowledge and LLM-based interpretation. Experiments on the mainstream benchmark across multiple prediction horizons and different temporal models show that Sera consistently improves forecasting performance, achieving up to a 37.3% reduction in prediction error over the temporal baseline and enhanced generalizability. Counterfactual analysis examines how forecasts respond to changes in degradation semantics to assess interpretability. The results show that prediction responses are consistent with the meanings of key degradation descriptors across tested horizons. Together, these findings demonstrate that structured degradation semantics and effective aggregation can improve forecasting accuracy and support reliable and interpretable battery health forecasting for advanced battery management.</p></details> |  |

Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. Within DM, generative learning and representation learning can be viewed as directionally inverse distributional processes: generative learning maps a tractable reference to data, whereas representation learning maps data to a designed reference law. | **[Approximation in Contrastive Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15388v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contrastive representation learning (CRL) is a fundamental paradigm for learning transferable representations. | **[FENAS: A Few-Shot Meta-Ensembling Framework for Efficient Neural Architecture Search with Synergized Zero-Cost Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15307v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient neural architecture search (NAS) requires accurate performance estimation under limited evaluation budgets. | **[Learning Contact Representation for Leg Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05501v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Leg odometry relies on the assumption that the velocity of a foot in stance is zero with respect to the world, which makes accurate contact detection a critical subproblem. | **[What Really Works for Universal Multimodal Embeddings? A Controlled Study of Contrastive Training Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15035v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contrastive learning has become the standard paradigm for multimodal representation learning, aligning semantically matched pairs while separating mismatched ones in a shared embedding space. Although various contrastive training strategies have been proposed across negative selection, batch construction, and gradient modulation, their actual effectiveness under controlled, reproducible conditions remains poorly understood. This motivates a fundamental question: Do sophisticated contrastive strategies consistently bring meaningful downstream gains in multimodal embedding learning? To address this, we systematically evaluate representative strategies under a unified benchmarking framework on MMEB. Surprisingly, direct evaluations reveal that most existing strategies offer marginal gains over a standard aligned baseline, with several even degrading overall performance. To uncover the underlying failure modes, we introduce an MLLM-as-a-Judge diagnostic framework that dissects top-1 retrieval behaviors into labeled positives, false negatives (unlabeled valid matches), and hard/easy negative errors. Our systematic analysis yields three key insights: (1) Gradient distortion: Upweighting gradients from high-similarity negatives consistently hurts downstream generalization; (2) Distribution illusion: Wider separation between positive and negative similarity distributions does not necessarily translate to better retrieval performance; and (3) Synergistic training: While false-negative mitigation or hard-batch construction yields limited gains in isolation, integrating hard-negative mining, false-negative mitigation, and hard-batch construction achieves optimal performance, delivering an average improvement of 6.0% on MMEB with Qwen2-VL-2B. Existing approaches rarely achieve both: discrete tokens ease modeling but lose fine-grained acoustic information, while continuous latents preserve fidelity but require specialized generative objectives. Submitted to ICASSP 2027</p></details> |
| **[PRISM: Precision Forcing for Generalist Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coupling a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) with a diffusion or flow-matching action expert has become the mainstream vision-language-action (VLA) paradigm for embodied policies, yet they remain unreliable once a task demands precise manipulation, e.g., submillimeter cable insertion. Along the generative path, inference integrates a locally supervised velocity field, so equally penalized errors at different flow times can affect the executed action unequally. Consequently, precision-task performance deteriorates as physical tolerance tightens by orders of magnitude. We call this supervision imbalance across contexts and flow times Flow-Time Starvation. The remedy is to jointly align supervision allocation and representation learning with context-dependent precision demand, enabling the policy to recognize when greater precision is needed. We instantiate this precision-forcing strategy as Precision-aware Reweighting and Internalization for Skilled Manipulation (PRISM), which scores precision demand offline, strengthens flow supervision where errors are consequential and action signals are visible, and internalizes that demand in the VLM condition representation. ## Graph Transformers
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Size-Transferability of Graph Transformers with Convolutional Positional Encodings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15239v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Transformers have achieved remarkable success across domains, motivating the rise of Graph Transformers (GTs) as attention-based architectures for graph-structured data. A key design choice in GTs is the use of Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based positional encodings to incorporate structural information. In this work, we study GTs through the lens of manifold limit models for graph sequences and establish a theoretical connection between GTs with GNN positional encodings and Manifold Neural Networks (MNNs). Building on transferability results for GNNs under manifold convergence, we show that GTs inherit transferability guarantees from their positional encodings. In particular, GTs trained on small graphs provably generalize to larger graphs under mild assumptions. We complement our theory with extensive experiments on standard graph benchmarks, demonstrating that GTs exhibit scalable behavior on par with GNNs. To further show the efficiency in a real-world scenario, we implement GTs for shortest path distance estimation over terrains to better illustrate the efficiency of the transferable GTs. Our results provide new insights into the understanding of GTs and suggest practical directions for efficient training of GTs in large-scale settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Coverage-Preserving Self-Healing in Partially Observable 3D UAV Swarm Networks via an Advection-Diffusion Graph Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22085v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates the resilience and self-healing of unmanned aerial vehicle swarm communication networks under severe network partitioning in complex and adversarial environments. To address limited global observability after damage, we develop a damage-state inference mechanism that enables the ground control station (GCS) to infer global failures from sparse local reports without transmitting location-sensitive coordinates. To recover multi-cluster partitioned topologies, we then propose AdDiff-GT, a physics-inspired graph Transformer that incorporates advection-diffusion dynamics into a node-supernode hybrid graph. Local message propagation captures intra-cluster structural relationships, while global attention models inter-cluster interactions. A weighted composite loss jointly considers connectivity restoration, maneuvering cost, motion consistency, inter-cluster coordination, and spatial preservation during model training. We further develop a meta-learning strategy for transferable initialization across damage patterns and swarm scales, enabling efficient online GCS adaptation and distributed airborne inference. Simulations on 8,000 partitioned scenarios, including 5,000 large-scale cases, show that AdDiff-GT achieves effective topology recovery and spatial preservation over six representative baselines. Meta-learning reduces online planning overhead with a minor increase in recovery steps, balancing prior knowledge transfer and online adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Physics-Aware Distillation of a Grid Foundation Model for Real-Time Edge Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21833v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Grid foundation models (GridFMs) are graph transformers pretrained on a masked power-flow objective, and evaluate power flow orders of magnitude faster than an iterative solver. Their size, however, targets data-centre inference, whereas low-inertia operation increasingly asks for inference at the edge, next to the measurement, at batch size one and within a single synchrophasor (PMU) reporting frame. We distil a 20.1M-parameter, 12-layer GridFM teacher into a 0.62M-parameter, 6-layer student, a $32.5\times$ parameter reduction at half the message-passing depth, using an objective that combines the masked power-flow loss with output, physics-residual, latent-state and attention transfer. The physics-residual term, which matches the teacher's power-residual profile, has not previously been applied to typed power-grid graphs. We evaluate on a renewable variant of the IEEE 118-bus benchmark grid in which 26 of the conventional (synchronous) generators are replaced by inverter-based renewables with battery flexibility and standardised reactive-power limits (IEEE 1547), giving $97\,529$ physically valid (AC-OPF-feasible) operating scenarios, and we ablate all eight combinations of the transfer terms, reporting accuracy against ground truth and fidelity to the teacher separately. Distillation lowers student error on all four predicted quantities, by up to $48\%$ on active-power dispatch, and every distilled student is more accurate than its teacher on reactive-power dispatch. The gain is largely carried by the output matching term. The physics-residual term, in the layer-aggregated form used here, is consistently harmful, which we attribute to the spatial averaging it applies to a bus-level residual field. The resulting student is $32.5\times$ smaller in parameter count and has half the message-passing depth, making it a candidate for subsequent edge-device benchmarking.</p></details> |  |
| **[Structural Properties of DomiRank Centrality: Symmetry Axioms, an Entropy Bound, and Spectral Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00107v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DomiRank is a node-importance algorithm for unweighted networks, defined by a dynamical model governed by a competition-strength parameter, a dominance threshold, and a decay rate. We establish three structural results. First, on symmetry. Automorphism-related vertices share the same DomiRank value, and a graph whose values are pairwise distinct must be asymmetric. The orbit count bounds the number of distinct values, and regular and vertex-transitive graphs admit closed forms. Second, on entropy. The maximum of the DomiRank entropy over connected graphs, log_2 N, is attained if and only if the graph is regular. The initial entropy derivative is the degree-log-degree edge covariance, making the decrease rigorous near vanishing competition for structurally disassortative graphs, and an assortative counterexample shows it does not extend to all graphs; a sufficient-condition theorem and a refined conjecture cover the rest. Third, on spectrum. The normalized distribution is sigma-invariant if and only if the degree vector is an adjacency-matrix eigenvector. The parameter sigma interpolates continuously between degree and smallest-eigenvector centrality within the resolvent (Bonacich-type) family, its sensitivity being a second-order suppression operator on the degree vector. Graph transformations are also characterized, including the m-product. We tackle the exponential explosion by proposing a heuristically minimal translation of the task into a well-established problem, set cover, for which highly optimized solutions exist. | **[CogChat: Knowledge Graph-Augmented Conversational AI with Heterogeneous Graph Transformer for Cognitive Grounding in Design Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13216v1)** | 2026-08-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based chat systems have become valuable tools for design practice, enabling rapid ideation and flexible task support. In design conversation, this gap compounds as relational context decays between turns, identical words go unresolved across designers, and the conversation loops or restarts rather than deepens. We present CogChat, a real-time chat framework that grounds conversational AI in a personal heterogeneous knowledge graph constructed from each designer's input. The system extracts typed entities and relations into a heterogeneous graph, then applies a HGT (Heterogeneous Graph Transformer) to select structurally relevant nodes for response generation and to generate both intentional and exploratory probing questions. Technical evaluation shows that HGT-based entity selection outperforms both ungrounded LLM interaction and naive KG augmentation, which introduces noise that degrades response quality. A within-subjects study with nine professional designers indicates that grounding conversation in a relationally structured, designer-specific semantic context improves context retention, personalized intent interpretation, and conversational depth while reducing cognitive load. These findings suggest that structuring a designer's expressed concepts and relations as a dynamic knowledge graph can preserve relational context that fades across turns, pointing toward a graph-grounded approach to long-term context management in LLM-based interaction.</p></details> |  |
| **[HSTGFormer: Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph Transformer for 3D Human Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12187v1)** | 2026-08-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Transformer-based methods have achieved strong performance in monocular 3D human pose estimation, but most existing approaches organise spatial and temporal reasoning as separate stages, which may weaken unified spatial-temporal interdependencies inherent in human motion and compress frame-level structural information before temporal modelling. Specifically, HSTGFormer introduces a Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph (HSTG), which decomposes global spatial-temporal reasoning into local spatial-temporal receptive fields around individual joint-time nodes by extending per-frame skeleton graphs into temporal neighbourhoods, thereby enabling structure-aware coupled reasoning while preserving local structural motion information. It further incorporates an Adaptive Dual-Scale Temporal Graph (ADSTG) to capture joint-specific temporal dependencies over complementary short- and long-range windows. A lightweight node-wise fusion module further adaptively integrates the two graph representations for each joint-time node. Experiments on Human3.6M and MPI-INF-3DHP show that HSTGFormer achieves strong accuracy with high computational efficiency.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to BMVC 2026, full paper</p></details> |
| **[Structural Morphisms for Nested Conditions - Full Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12096v1)** | 2026-08-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Nested conditions are used, among other things, as a graphical way to express first order formulas ruling the applicability of a graph transformation rule to a given match. Finally we frame the results in a categorical context, proving functoriality and universality properties of the various operations.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This is the full version (including proofs) of the paper published in GCM 2026</p></details> |
| **[BiScale-GTR: Fragment-Aware Graph Transformers for Multi-Scale Molecular Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06336v2)** | 2026-08-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fragment-level representations provide a natural way to capture recurring molecular substructures and reuse their learned representations across molecules. Each shared fragment token is then grounded with atom-level GNN representations through atom-to-fragment pooling and gated fusion, allowing the same fragment identity to acquire context-dependent representations in different molecular environments. A structure-aware fragment Transformer performs global reasoning over these atom-grounded tokens, capturing reusable substructure identity, local chemical context, and long-range molecular dependencies. Experiments on MoleculeNet, PharmaBench, and the Long Range Graph Benchmark demonstrate strong performance across classification and regression tasks. Attribution analysis further shows that BiScale-GTR highlights chemically meaningful recurring motifs, providing interpretable links between molecular structure and predicted properties.</p></details> |  |
| **[Order Matters in Retrosynthesis: Structure-aware Generation via Reaction-Center-Guided Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13136v2)** | 2026-08-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Template-free retrosynthesis methods treat the task as black-box sequence generation, limiting learning efficiency, while semi-template approaches rely on rigid reaction libraries that constrain generalization. By placing reaction center atoms at the sequence head, our method transforms implicit chemical knowledge into explicit positional patterns that the model can readily capture. Combined with discrete flow matching, our approach decouples training from sampling and enables generation in 20--50 steps versus 500 for prior diffusion methods. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance on both USPTO-50k (61.2% top-1) and the large-scale USPTO-Full (51.3% top-1) with predicted reaction centers. Our method explicitly models overpasses and uses topology-preserving vertex extraction to mitigate routing-critical errors. This approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on City-scale, SpaceNet, and Global-scale benchmarks in topology metrics including TOPO-F1 and APLS. In addition to quantitative gains, our method improves reconstruction of multi-level road structures crucial for real-world routing reliability. | **[Cluster Attention for Graph Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07492v2)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Message Passing Neural Networks have recently become the most popular approach to graph machine learning tasks; however, their receptive field is limited by the number of message passing layers. To increase the receptive field, Graph Transformers with global attention have been proposed; however, global attention does not take into account the graph topology and thus lacks graph-structure-based inductive biases, which are typically very important for graph machine learning tasks. In this work, we propose an alternative approach: cluster attention (CLATT). We divide graph nodes into clusters with off-the-shelf graph community detection algorithms and let each node attend to all other nodes in each cluster. CLATT provides large receptive fields while still having strong graph-structure-based inductive biases. We show that augmenting Message Passing Neural Networks or Graph Transformers with CLATT significantly improves their performance on a wide range of graph datasets including datasets from the recently introduced GraphLand benchmark representing real-world applications of graph machine learning.</p></details> | minor fixes |
| **[Accounting Graph Transformer for Short-History Multi-KPI Forecasting in Small Businesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07037v1)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Small businesses often have only 12-24 months of accounting history, yet planning and risk workflows require coordinated forecasts across financial statements. Across 11,993 forecast origins from 1,060 unseen companies, AGT achieves sample-weighted KPI-macro mean absolute error (MAE) $0.6990 \pm 0.0013$ over three independent seeds, compared with $0.7378 \pm 0.0014$ for the strongest baseline, LightGBM. At the pre-specified seed 42, a paired company-clustered bootstrap gives a LightGBM-minus-AGT difference of 0.0395 with 95% confidence interval (CI) $[0.0350,0.0439]$. AGT is best on all 13 KPIs against LightGBM, TimeMixer, and SOFTS in the matched seed-42 comparison, while final-architecture ablations show that relational attention, accounting topology, and the recency path each improve validation and test accuracy. On 7,094 additional unseen companies with origins sampled from January-May 2025, AGT obtains 0.7548 MAE versus 0.7694 for SOFTS. | **[MiGHT-EHR: A Multi-task Graph Transformer for Heterogeneous Temporal Electronic Health Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06430v1)** | 2026-08-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning from Electronic Health Records (EHRs) has gained significant attention due to its potential to improve clinical prediction. To bridge this gap, we propose Multi-task Graph transformer for Heterogeneous Temporal EHRs (MiGHT-EHR), which jointly models all three within a unified representation learning method. Furthermore, a post-hoc analysis of the learned representations reveals that patient neighborhoods are organized by clinical outcomes, salient medical concepts are recoverable as linear directions in the representation space, and task probabilities are well calibrated.
